## Chapter 8: The Principle of Vision

**The Myth of Sisyphus.** The philosophical treatment of absurdity and the heroism of continued effort: Camus, A. (1942). *Le Mythe de Sisyphe*. Gallimard. English translation: Camus, A. (1955). *The Myth of Sisyphus and Other Essays*. Trans. Justin O’Brien. Hamish Hamilton.

**Maxwell’s Demon.** The thought experiment establishing that information processing has thermodynamic costs: Maxwell, J. C. (1871). *Theory of Heat*. Longmans, Green and Co. For the resolution establishing that sorting information requires energy expenditure: Landauer, R. (1961). “Irreversibility and heat generation in the computing process.” *IBM Journal of Research and Development*, 5(3), 183–191.

**Transgenerational Epigenetic Inheritance.** The documentation of environmental experience altering gene expression across generations: Lumey, L. H., et al. (2007). “Cohort profile: The Dutch Hunger Winter families study.” *International Journal of Epidemiology*, 36(6), 1196–1204. For the broader framework: Skinner, M. K. (2014). “Environmental epigenetics and a unified theory of the molecular aspects of disease: A systems approach.” *Genetics*, 198(2), 400–416.

**Ergodicity and Absorbing Barriers.** The application of ergodicity to individual decision-making and ruin avoidance: Peters, O. (2019). “The ergodicity problem in economics.” *Nature Physics*, 15(12), 1216–1221. For the absorbing barrier concept applied to financial and strategic decision-making: Taleb, N. N. (2018). *Skin in the Game: Hidden Asymmetries in Daily Life*. Random House.

**Thermodynamics and Life as Negentropy.** The foundational framing of living organisms as local reversals of entropy: Schrödinger, E. (1944). *What Is Life? The Physical Aspect of the Living Cell*. Cambridge University Press.

**The Lindy Effect.** (See Chapter 7 citation: Taleb, 2018.)

## Chapter 9: Forging the Maha Individual

**Melanopsin and Circadian Photoreception.** The identification of melanopsin-containing intrinsically photosensitive retinal ganglion cells (ipRGCs) and their role in communicating light information to the suprachiasmatic nucleus: Provencio, I., et al. (2000). “A novel human opsin in the inner retina.” *Journal of Neuroscience*, 20(2), 600–605. For the clinical implications of evening blue light exposure: Chang, A. M., et al. (2015). “Evening use of light-emitting eReaders negatively affects sleep, circadian timing, and next-morning alertness.” *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences*, 112(4), 1232–1237.

**The Default Mode Network and Morning Vulnerability.** On DMN architecture and its role in integrative cognition during rest: Buckner, R. L., Andrews-Hanna, J. R., & Schacter, D. L. (2008). “The brain’s default network: Anatomy, function, and relevance to disease.” *Annals of the New York Academy of Sciences*, 1124(1), 1–38. For the colonisation of resting cognition by digital content and its downstream attentional effects: see Leroy, S. (2009) and Mark, G. (2023) previously cited in Chapter 2 notes.

**Hormesis.** The biological framework for adaptive response to controlled acute stressors: Calabrese, E. J., & Baldwin, L. A. (2002). “Defining hormesis.” *Human and Experimental Toxicology*, 21(2), 91–97. For hormesis applied to exercise physiology: Radák, Z., et al. (2005). “Exercise, free radicals, and antioxidants.” *Comprehensive Physiology*, previously cited as Taleb antifragility principle; see also Mattson, M. P. (2008). “Hormesis defined.” *Aging Research Reviews*, 7(1), 1–7.

**Cold Exposure and Dopamine.** Research on physiological responses to cold water immersion — full-body exposure at 8–14°C — documenting significant increases in noradrenaline and meaningful increases in plasma dopamine: Šrámek, P., et al. (2000). “Human physiological responses to immersion into water of different temperatures.” *European Journal of Applied Physiology*, 81(5), 436–442. The Chapter 9 protocol applies the same neurochemical principle via brief cold showers; full-body immersion produces more precisely quantified effects than the shower protocol specifically.

## Chapter 10: Weaving the Maha Community

**Ostrom’s Design Principles for Common-Pool Resources.** The Nobel Prize-winning research demonstrating that local communities can govern shared resources sustainably without privatisation or central control: Ostrom, E. (1990). *Governing the Commons: The Evolution of Institutions for Collective Action*. Cambridge University Press. For a summary of the eight design principles: Cox, M., Arnold, G., & Villamayor-Tomás, S. (2010). “A review of design principles for community-based natural resource management.” *Ecology and Society*, 15(4), 38.

**The Overton Window.** The concept of the politically acceptable range of policy options and how it shifts: Overton, J. P. (1993). Developed at the Mackinac Center for Public Policy. For the mechanism of local action shifting national policy windows: see the broader literature on policy diffusion and policy windows in: Kingdon, J. W. (1984). *Agendas, Alternatives, and Public Policies*. Little, Brown.

**Metcalfe’s Law.** The network value scaling principle: Metcalfe, R. M., & Boggs, D. R. (1976). “Ethernet: Distributed packet switching for local computer networks.” *Communications of the ACM*, 19(7), 395–404. For the application of network effects to social and civic organization: Shapiro, C., & Varian, H. R. (1999). *Information Rules: A Strategic Guide to the Network Economy*. Harvard Business School Press.

**Dunbar’s Number.** (See Chapter 3 citation: Dunbar, 1992.)

**The Philosophy of the Face.** The ethical argument that genuine moral obligation arises from direct physical encounter with another person: Levinas, E. (1969). *Totality and Infinity: An Essay on Exteriority*. Trans. Alphonso Lingis. Duquesne University Press.

**Subsidiarity and the Little Platoon.** On the primacy of local civic association as the foundation of broader civic participation: de Tocqueville, A. (1835, 1840). *Democracy in America*. Various editions. The specific concept of the “little platoon” as the first expression of public affections: Burke, E. (1790). *Reflections on the Revolution in France*.

**Collective Effervescence.** The sociological concept of shared emotional states produced by collective physical activity: Durkheim, É. (1912). *The Elementary Forms of Religious Life*. Trans. Karen Fields (1995). Free Press.

## Chapter 11: Architecting the Maha Nation

**Regulatory Capture.** The formal economic theory of regulatory failure in which regulated industries come to dominate the agencies overseeing them: Stigler, G. J. (1971). “The theory of economic regulation.” *Bell Journal of Economics and Management Science*, 2(1), 3–21. For the contemporary revolving door mechanism: Moss, D., & Cisternino, J. (Eds.) (2009). *New Perspectives on Regulation*. The Tobin Project.

**Actuarial Ruin.** The actuarial science framework for insolvency triggered by claim frequency exceeding model assumptions: see standard actuarial literature including: Bowers, N. L., et al. (1997). *Actuarial Mathematics* (2nd ed.). Society of Actuaries. For the metabolic disease burden on insurance actuarial models: see the American Academy of Actuaries publications on chronic disease and long-term care cost projections.

**Kessler Syndrome.** The original formulation of orbital debris cascade: Kessler, D. J., & Cour-Palais, B. G. (1978). “Collision frequency of artificial satellites: The creation of a debris belt.” *Journal of Geophysical Research: Space Physics*, 83(A6), 2637–2646. The Kessler Syndrome of the Mind is the Maha Principle’s original application of this cascade mechanics to the attention economy.

**Population-Level Testosterone Decline.** The documented decline in serum testosterone across American men over the twentieth century: Travison, T. G., et al. (2007). “A Population-Level Decline in Serum Testosterone Levels in American Men.” *The Journal of Clinical Endocrinology & Metabolism*, 92(1), 196–202.

**Cincinnatus.** The historical account of Lucius Quinctius Cincinnatus is drawn primarily from: Livy. *Ab Urbe Condita* (History of Rome), Book III (c. 27–9 BCE).

**The Public Trust Doctrine.** The legal theory holding certain natural resources in public trust against private destruction: Sax, J. L. (1970). “The Public Trust Doctrine in Natural Resource Law: Effective Judicial Intervention.” *Michigan Law Review*, 68(3), 471–566.

**Edmund Burke on Generational Stewardship.** The partnership between the living, the dead, and the unborn: Burke, E. (1790). *Reflections on the Revolution in France*.

**The Heckman Curve.** (See Chapter 6 citation: Heckman, 2006.)

**The Great Filter.** The formulation of the Great Filter as the explanation for the Fermi Paradox: Hanson, R. (1998). “The Great Filter — Are We Almost Past It?” \[Working paper\]. Retrieved from mason.gmu.edu/~rhanson/greatfilter.html.

## A Note on Evidence Standards

The Maha Principle draws on research across multiple disciplines — nutritional epidemiology, neuroscience, political philosophy, ecology, and evolutionary biology. These fields operate with different evidentiary standards and different levels of scientific consensus.

Where the book makes specific quantitative claims — metabolic health statistics, attention recovery times, loneliness mortality equivalents — the underlying research is cited above and readers are encouraged to consult the primary sources.

Where the book makes mechanistic claims about causation — the role of seed oils in inflammation, the effects of digital feeds on executive function — the evidence cited is in most cases suggestive rather than definitive. The Falsifiability Protocol in Appendix C is explicit about what evidence would require the book to revise these claims.

Where the book draws on philosophy, history, and political theory, the sources cited are the works from which the specific arguments are drawn, not claims about empirical consensus.

The distinction between what is established, what is probable, and what is argued is the reader’s responsibility to maintain — and this book’s responsibility to make as clear as possible.
